You are Assessment A of an `$impeccable critique`: an independent LLM design review, done like a design director, of the whole "Read, Recall, Colour" (RRC) drill in the LSAT U web app. You must not see or ask for any other assessment's output. Do not edit any project files. Return structured findings as text.

## What the interface is trying to accomplish
RRC is a spoken LSAT drill. A student opens a door page, picks a section (22 to 26 Logical Reasoning stimuli), reads a briefing, presses Start, and then runs the whole section in one recorded take against a 25-minute goal: for each stimulus she reads it out loud in pieces, tells each piece back in her own words, "colours it" (says her own reaction), then presses Space, the text vanishes and she recalls the whole argument from memory, then presses Space for the next one. Nothing is judged live. After the run, an AI grader marks it and she gets a Report Card: what she carried, what she dropped, her own recording beside the standard, line by line, and what to do next time. The students are paying one-to-one coaching clients of Joshua (the sole instructor), mostly on laptops, in 25-minute focused sittings, often at night. There is one real student's incident on record: a run whose recorder started 37 minutes early and a grade stuck "still being marked" for 8 hours.

# Heuristics Scoring Guide

Score each of Nielsen's 10 Usability Heuristics on a 0–4 scale. Be honest — a 4 means genuinely excellent, not "good enough."

## Nielsen's 10 Heuristics

### 1. Visibility of System Status

Keep users informed about what's happening through timely, appropriate feedback.

**Check for**:
- Loading indicators during async operations
- Confirmation of user actions (save, submit, delete)
- Progress indicators for multi-step processes
- Current location in navigation (breadcrumbs, active states)
- Form validation feedback (inline, not just on submit)

**Scoring**:
| Score | Criteria |
|-------|----------|
| 0 | No feedback — user is guessing what happened |
| 1 | Rare feedback — most actions produce no visible response |
| 2 | Partial — some states communicated, major gaps remain |
| 3 | Good — most operations give clear feedback, minor gaps |
| 4 | Excellent — every action confirms, progress is always visible |

### 2. Match Between System and Real World

Speak the user's language. Follow real-world conventions. Information appears in natural, logical order.

**Check for**:
- Familiar terminology (no unexplained jargon)
- Logical information order matching user expectations
- Recognizable icons and metaphors
- Domain-appropriate language for the target audience
- Natural reading flow (left-to-right, top-to-bottom priority)

**Scoring**:
| Score | Criteria |
|-------|----------|
| 0 | Pure tech jargon, alien to users |
| 1 | Mostly confusing — requires domain expertise to navigate |
| 2 | Mixed — some plain language, some jargon leaks through |
| 3 | Mostly natural — occasional term needs context |
| 4 | Speaks the user's language fluently throughout |

### 3. User Control and Freedom

Users need a clear "emergency exit" from unwanted states without extended dialogue.

**Check for**:
- Undo/redo functionality
- Cancel buttons on forms and modals
...

235	# Cognitive Load Assessment
236	
237	Cognitive load is the total mental effort required to use an interface. Overloaded users make mistakes, get frustrated, and leave. This reference helps identify and fix cognitive overload.
238	
239	---
240	
241	## Three Types of Cognitive Load
242	
243	### Intrinsic Load — The Task Itself
244	Complexity inherent to what the user is trying to do. You can't eliminate this, but you can structure it.
245	
246	**Manage it by**:
247	- Breaking complex tasks into discrete steps
248	- Providing scaffolding (templates, defaults, examples)
249	- Progressive disclosure — show what's needed now, hide the rest
250	- Grouping related decisions together
251	
252	### Extraneous Load — Bad Design
253	Mental effort caused by poor design choices. **Eliminate this ruthlessly** — it's pure waste.
254	
255	**Common sources**:
256	- Confusing navigation that requires mental mapping
257	- Unclear labels that force users to guess meaning
258	- Visual clutter competing for attention
259	- Inconsistent patterns that prevent learning
260	- Unnecessary steps between user intent and result
261	
262	### Germane Load — Learning Effort
263	Mental effort spent building understanding. This is *good* cognitive load — it leads to mastery.
264	
265	**Support it by**:
266	- Progressive disclosure that reveals complexity gradually
267	- Consistent patterns that reward learning
268	- Feedback that confirms correct understanding
269	- Onboarding that teaches through action, not walls of text
270	
271	---
272	
273	## Cognitive Load Checklist
274	
275	Evaluate the interface against these 8 items:
276	
277	- [ ] **Single focus**: Can the user complete their primary task without distraction from competing elements?
278	- [ ] **Chunking**: Is information presented in digestible groups (≤4 items per group)?
279	- [ ] **Grouping**: Are related items visually grouped together (proximity, borders, shared background)?
280	- [ ] **Visual hierarchy**: Is it immediately clear what's most important on the screen?
281	- [ ] **One thing at a time**: Can the user focus on a single decision before moving to the next?
282	- [ ] **Minimal choices**: Are decisions simplified (≤4 visible options at any decision point)?
283	- [ ] **Working memory**: Does the user need to remember information from a previous screen to act on the current one?
284	- [ ] **Progressive disclosure**: Is complexity revealed only when the user needs it?
285	
286	**Scoring**: Count the failed items. 0–1 failures = low cognitive load (good). 2–3 = moderate (address soon). 4+ = high cognitive load (critical fix needed).
287	
288	---
289	
290	## The Working Memory Rule
291	
292	**Humans can hold ≤4 items in working memory at once** (Miller's Law revised by Cowan, 2001).
293	
294	At any decision point, count the number of distinct options, actions, or pieces of information a user must simultaneously consider:
295	- **≤4 items**: Within working memory limits — manageable
296	- **5–7 items**: Pushing the boundary — consider grouping or progressive disclosure
297	- **8+ items**: Overloaded — users will skip, misclick, or abandon
298	
299	**Practical applications**:
300	- Navigation menus: ≤5 top-level items (group the rest under clear categories)
301	- Form sections: ≤4 fields visible per group before a visual break
302	- Action buttons: 1 primary, 1–2 secondary, group the rest in a menu
303	- Dashboard widgets: ≤4 key metrics visible without scrolling
304	- Pricing tiers: ≤3 options (more causes analysis paralysis)
305	
306	---
307	
308	## Common Cognitive Load Violations
309	
310	### 1. The Wall of Options
311	**Problem**: Presenting 10+ choices at once with no hierarchy.
312	**Fix**: Group into categories, highlight recommended, use progressive disclosure.
313	
314	### 2. The Memory Bridge
315	**Problem**: User must remember info from step 1 to complete step 3.
316	**Fix**: Keep relevant context visible, or repeat it where it's needed.
317	
318	### 3. The Hidden Navigation
319	**Problem**: User must build a mental map of where things are.
320	**Fix**: Always show current location (breadcrumbs, active states, progress indicators).
321	
322	### 4. The Jargon Barrier
323	**Problem**: Technical or domain language forces translation effort.
324	**Fix**: Use plain language. If domain terms are unavoidable, define them inline.
325	
326	### 5. The Visual Noise Floor
327	**Problem**: Every element has the same visual weight — nothing stands out.
328	**Fix**: Establish clear hierarchy: one primary element, 2–3 secondary, everything else muted.
329	
330	### 6. The Inconsistent Pattern
331	**Problem**: Similar actions work differently in different places.
332	**Fix**: Standardize interaction patterns. Same type of action = same type of UI.
333	
334	### 7. The Multi-Task Demand
335	**Problem**: Interface requires processing multiple simultaneous inputs (reading + deciding + navigating).
336	**Fix**: Sequence the steps. Let the user do one thing at a time.
337	
338	### 8. The Context Switch
339	**Problem**: User must jump between screens/tabs/modals to gather info for a single decision.
340	**Fix**: Co-locate the information needed for each decision. Reduce back-and-forth.
341	# Persona-Based Design Testing
342	
343	Test the interface through the eyes of 5 distinct user archetypes. Each persona exposes different failure modes that a single "design director" perspective would miss.
344	
345	**How to use**: Select 2–3 personas most relevant to the interface being critiqued. Walk through the primary user action as each persona. Report specific red flags — not generic concerns.
346	
347	---
348	
349	## 1. Impatient Power User — "Alex"
350	
351	**Profile**: Expert with similar products. Expects efficiency, hates hand-holding. Will find shortcuts or leave.
352	
353	**Behaviors**:
354	- Skips all onboarding and instructions
355	- Looks for keyboard shortcuts immediately
356	- Tries to bulk-select, batch-edit, and automate
357	- Gets frustrated by required steps that feel unnecessary
358	- Abandons if anything feels slow or patronizing
359	
360	**Test Questions**:
361	- Can Alex complete the core task in under 60 seconds?
362	- Are there keyboard shortcuts for common actions?
363	- Can onboarding be skipped entirely?
364	- Do modals have keyboard dismiss (Esc)?
365	- Is there a "power user" path (shortcuts, bulk actions)?
366	
367	**Red Flags** (report these specifically):
368	- Forced tutorials or unskippable onboarding
369	- No keyboard navigation for primary actions
370	- Slow animations that can't be skipped
371	- One-item-at-a-time workflows where batch would be natural
372	- Redundant confirmation steps for low-risk actions
373	
374	---
375	
376	## 2. Confused First-Timer — "Jordan"
377	
378	**Profile**: Never used this type of product. Needs guidance at every step. Will abandon rather than figure it out.
379	
380	**Behaviors**:
381	- Reads all instructions carefully
382	- Hesitates before clicking anything unfamiliar
383	- Looks for help or support constantly
384	- Misunderstands jargon and abbreviations
385	- Takes the most literal interpretation of any label
386	
387	**Test Questions**:
388	- Is the first action obviously clear within 5 seconds?
389	- Are all icons labeled with text?
390	- Is there contextual help at decision points?
391	- Does terminology assume prior knowledge?
392	- Is there a clear "back" or "undo" at every step?
393	
394	**Red Flags** (report these specifically):
395	- Icon-only navigation with no labels
396	- Technical jargon without explanation
397	- No visible help option or guidance
398	- Ambiguous next steps after completing an action
399	- No confirmation that an action succeeded
400	
401	---
402	
403	## 3. Accessibility-Dependent User — "Sam"
404	
405	**Profile**: Uses screen reader (VoiceOver/NVDA), keyboard-only navigation. May have low vision, motor impairment, or cognitive differences.
406	
407	**Behaviors**:
408	- Tabs through the interface linearly
409	- Relies on ARIA labels and heading structure
410	- Cannot see hover states or visual-only indicators
411	- Needs adequate color contrast (4.5:1 minimum)
412	- May use browser zoom up to 200%
413	
414	**Test Questions**:
415	- Can the entire primary flow be completed keyboard-only?
416	- Are all interactive elements focusable with visible focus indicators?
417	- Do images have meaningful alt text?
418	- Is color contrast WCAG AA compliant (4.5:1 for text)?
419	- Does the screen reader announce state changes (loading, success, errors)?
420	
421	**Red Flags** (report these specifically):
422	- Click-only interactions with no keyboard alternative
423	- Missing or invisible focus indicators
424	- Meaning conveyed by color alone (red = error, green = success)
425	- Unlabeled form fields or buttons
426	- Time-limited actions without extension option
427	- Custom components that break screen reader flow
428	
429	---
430	
431	## 4. Deliberate Stress Tester — "Riley"
432	
433	**Profile**: Methodical user who pushes interfaces beyond the happy path. Tests edge cases, tries unexpected inputs, and probes for gaps in the experience.
434	
435	**Behaviors**:
436	- Tests edge cases intentionally (empty states, long strings, special characters)
437	- Submits forms with unexpected data (emoji, RTL text, very long values)
438	- Tries to break workflows by navigating backwards, refreshing mid-flow, or opening in multiple tabs
439	- Looks for inconsistencies between what the UI promises and what actually happens
440	- Documents problems methodically
441	
442	**Test Questions**:
443	- What happens at the edges (0 items, 1000 items, very long text)?
444	- Do error states recover gracefully or leave the UI in a broken state?
445	- What happens on refresh mid-workflow? Is state preserved?
446	- Are there features that appear to work but produce broken results?
447	- How does the UI handle unexpected input (emoji, special chars, paste from Excel)?
448	
449	**Red Flags** (report these specifically):
450	- Features that appear to work but silently fail or produce wrong results
451	- Error handling that exposes technical details or leaves UI in a broken state
452	- Empty states that show nothing useful ("No results" with no guidance)
453	- Workflows that lose user data on refresh or navigation
454	- Inconsistent behavior between similar interactions in different parts of the UI
455	
456	---
457	
458	## 5. Distracted Mobile User — "Casey"
459	
460	**Profile**: Using phone one-handed on the go. Frequently interrupted. Possibly on a slow connection.
461	
462	**Behaviors**:
463	- Uses thumb only — prefers bottom-of-screen actions
464	- Gets interrupted mid-flow and returns later
465	- Switches between apps frequently
466	- Has limited attention span and low patience
467	- Types as little as possible, prefers taps and selections
468	
469	**Test Questions**:
470	- Are primary actions in the thumb zone (bottom half of screen)?
471	- Is state preserved if the user leaves and returns?
472	- Does it work on slow connections (3G)?
473	- Can forms leverage autocomplete and smart defaults?
474	- Are touch targets at least 44×44pt?
475	
476	**Red Flags** (report these specifically):
477	- Important actions positioned at the top of the screen (unreachable by thumb)
478	- No state persistence — progress lost on tab switch or interruption
479	- Large text inputs required where selection would work
480	- Heavy assets loading on every page (no lazy loading)
481	- Tiny tap targets or targets too close together
482	
483	---
484	
485	## Selecting Personas
486	
487	Choose personas based on the interface type:
488	
489	| Interface Type | Primary Personas | Why |
490	|---------------|-----------------|-----|
491	| Landing page / marketing | Jordan, Riley, Casey | First impressions, trust, mobile |
492	| Dashboard / admin | Alex, Sam | Power users, accessibility |
493	| E-commerce / checkout | Casey, Riley, Jordan | Mobile, edge cases, clarity |
494	| Onboarding flow | Jordan, Casey | Confusion, interruption |
495	| Data-heavy / analytics | Alex, Sam | Efficiency, keyboard nav |
496	| Form-heavy / wizard | Jordan, Sam, Casey | Clarity, accessibility, mobile |
497	
498	---
499	
500	## Project-Specific Personas
501	
502	If `AGENTS.md` contains a `## Design Context` section (generated by `impeccable teach`), derive 1–2 additional personas from the audience and brand information:
503	
504	1. Read the target audience description
505	2. Identify the primary user archetype not covered by the 5 predefined personas
506	3. Create a persona following this template:
507	
508	```
509	### [Role] — "[Name]"
510	
511	**Profile**: [2-3 key characteristics derived from Design Context]
512	
513	**Behaviors**: [3-4 specific behaviors based on the described audience]
514	
515	**Red Flags**: [3-4 things that would alienate this specific user type]
516	```
517	
518	Only generate project-specific personas when real Design Context data is available. Don't invent audience details — use the 5 predefined personas when no context exists.
519	# Product register
520	
521	When design SERVES the product: app UIs, admin dashboards, settings panels, data tables, tools, authenticated surfaces, anything where the user is in a task.
522	
523	## The product slop test
524	
525	Not "would someone say AI made this" — familiarity is often a feature here. The test is: would a user fluent in the category's best tools (Linear, Figma, Notion, Raycast, Stripe come to mind) sit down and trust this interface, or pause at every subtly-off component?
526	
527	Product UI's failure mode isn't flatness, it's strangeness without purpose: over-decorated buttons, mismatched form controls, gratuitous motion, display fonts where labels should be, invented affordances for standard tasks. The bar is earned familiarity. The tool should disappear into the task.
528	
529	## Typography
530	
531	- **System fonts are legitimate.** `-apple-system, BlinkMacSystemFont, "Segoe UI", system-ui, sans-serif` gives you native feel on every platform. Inter is the common cross-platform default for a reason.
532	- **One family is often right.** Product UIs don't need display/body pairing. A well-tuned sans carries headings, buttons, labels, body, data.
533	- **Fixed rem scale, not fluid.** Clamp-sized headings don't serve product UI. Users view at consistent DPI, and a fluid h1 that shrinks in a sidebar looks worse, not better.
534	- **Tighter scale ratio.** 1.125–1.2 between steps is typical. More type elements here than on brand surfaces; exaggerated contrast creates noise.
535	- **Line length still applies for prose** (65–75ch). Data and compact UI can run denser — tables at 120ch+ are fine.
536	
537	## Color
538	
539	Product defaults to Restrained. A single surface can earn Committed — a dashboard where one category color carries a report, an onboarding flow with a drenched welcome screen — but Restrained is the floor.
540	
541	- State-rich semantic vocabulary: hover, focus, active, disabled, selected, loading, error, warning, success, info. Standardize these.
542	- Accent color used for primary actions, current selection, and state indicators only — not decoration.
543	- A second neutral layer for sidebars, toolbars, and panels (slightly cooler or warmer than the content surface).
544	
545	## Layout
546	
547	- Predictable grids. Consistency IS an affordance — users navigate faster when the structure is expected.
548	- Familiar patterns are features. Standard navigation (top bar, side nav), breadcrumbs, tabs, and form layouts have established user expectations. Don't reinvent for flavor.
549	- Responsive behavior is structural (collapse sidebar, responsive table, breakpoint-driven columns), not fluid typography.
550	
551	## Components
552	
553	Every interactive component has: default, hover, focus, active, disabled, loading, error. Don't ship with half of these.
554	
555	- Skeleton states for loading, not spinners in the middle of content.
556	- Empty states that teach the interface, not "nothing here."
557	- Consistent affordances across the surface. Same button shape. Same form-control vocabulary. Same icon style.
558	
559	## Motion
560	
561	- 150–250 ms on most transitions. Users are in flow — don't make them wait for choreography.
562	- Motion conveys state, not decoration. State change, feedback, loading, reveal — nothing else.
563	- No orchestrated page-load sequences. Product loads into a task; users don't want to watch it load.
564	
565	## Product bans (on top of the shared absolute bans)
566	
567	- Decorative motion that doesn't convey state.
568	- Inconsistent component vocabulary across screens. If the "save" button looks different in two places, one is wrong.
569	- Display fonts in UI labels, buttons, data.
570	- Reinventing standard affordances for flavor (custom scrollbars, weird form controls, non-standard modals).
571	- Heavy color or full-saturation accents on inactive states.
572	
573	## Product permissions
574	
575	Product can afford things brand surfaces can't.
576	
577	- System fonts and familiar sans defaults (Inter, SF Pro, system-ui stacks).
578	- Standard navigation patterns: top bar + side nav, breadcrumbs, tabs, command palettes.
579	- Density. Tables with many rows, panels with many labels, dense information when users need it.
580	- Consistency over surprise. The same visual vocabulary screen to screen is a virtue; delight is saved for moments, not pages.
581	======SKILL
582	## Shared design laws
583	
584	Apply to every design, both registers. Match implementation complexity to the aesthetic vision — maximalism needs elaborate code, minimalism needs precision. Interpret creatively. Vary across projects; never converge on the same choices. GPT is capable of extraordinary work — don't hold back.
585	
586	### Color
587	
588	- Use OKLCH. Reduce chroma as lightness approaches 0 or 100 — high chroma at extremes looks garish.
589	- Never use `#000` or `#fff`. Tint every neutral toward the brand hue (chroma 0.005–0.01 is enough).
590	- Pick a **color strategy** before picking colors. Four steps on the commitment axis:
591	  - **Restrained** — tinted neutrals + one accent ≤10%. Product default; brand minimalism.
592	  - **Committed** — one saturated color carries 30–60% of the surface. Brand default for identity-driven pages.
593	  - **Full palette** — 3–4 named roles, each used deliberately. Brand campaigns; product data viz.
594	  - **Drenched** — the surface IS the color. Brand heroes, campaign pages.
595	- The "one accent ≤10%" rule is Restrained only. Committed / Full palette / Drenched exceed it on purpose. Don't collapse every design to Restrained by reflex.
596	
597	### Theme
598	
599	Dark vs. light is never a default. Not dark "because tools look cool dark." Not light "to be safe."
600	
601	Before choosing, write one sentence of physical scene: who uses this, where, under what ambient light, in what mood. If the sentence doesn't force the answer, it's not concrete enough — add detail until it does.
602	
603	"Observability dashboard" does not force an answer. "SRE glancing at incident severity on a 27-inch monitor at 2am in a dim room" does. Run the sentence, not the category.
604	
605	### Typography
606	
607	- Cap body line length at 65–75ch.
608	- Hierarchy through scale + weight contrast (≥1.25 ratio between steps). Avoid flat scales.
609	
610	### Layout
611	
612	- Vary spacing for rhythm. Same padding everywhere is monotony.
613	- Cards are the lazy answer. Use them only when they're truly the best affordance. Nested cards are always wrong.
614	- Don't wrap everything in a container. Most things don't need one.
615	
616	### Motion
617	
618	- Don't animate CSS layout properties.
619	- Ease out with exponential curves (ease-out-quart / quint / expo). No bounce, no elastic.
620	
621	### Absolute bans
622	
623	Match-and-refuse. If you're about to write any of these, rewrite the element with different structure.
624	
625	- **Side-stripe borders.** `border-left` or `border-right` greater than 1px as a colored accent on cards, list items, callouts, or alerts. Never intentional. Rewrite with full borders, background tints, leading numbers/icons, or nothing.
626	- **Gradient text.** `background-clip: text` combined with a gradient background. Decorative, never meaningful. Use a single solid color. Emphasis via weight or size.
627	- **Glassmorphism as default.** Blurs and glass cards used decoratively. Rare and purposeful, or nothing.
628	- **The hero-metric template.** Big number, small label, supporting stats, gradient accent. SaaS cliché.
629	- **Identical card grids.** Same-sized cards with icon + heading + text, repeated endlessly.
630	- **Modal as first thought.** Modals are usually laziness. Exhaust inline / progressive alternatives first.
631	
632	### Copy
633	
634	- Every word earns its place. No restated headings, no intros that repeat the title.
635	- **No em dashes.** Use commas, colons, semicolons, periods, or parentheses. Also not `--`.
636	
637	### The AI slop test
638	
639	If someone could look at this interface and say "AI made that" without doubt, it's failed. Cross-register failures are the absolute bans above. Register-specific failures live in each reference.
640	
641	**Category-reflex check.** Run at two altitudes — the second one catches what the first one misses.
642	
643	- **First-order:** if someone could guess the theme + palette from the category alone — "observability → dark blue", "healthcare → white + teal", "finance → navy + gold", "crypto → neon on black" — it's the first training-data reflex. Rework the scene sentence and color strategy until the answer isn't obvious from the domain.
644	- **Second-order:** if someone could guess the aesthetic family from category-plus-anti-references — "AI workflow tool that's not SaaS-cream → editorial-typographic", "fintech that's not navy-and-gold → terminal-native dark mode" — it's the trap one tier deeper. The first reflex was avoided; the second wasn't. Rework until both answers are not obvious. The brand register's [reflex-reject aesthetic lanes](reference/brand.md) list catches the currently-saturated families.
645

## assistant

Now the repo context: PRODUCT.md, DESIGN.md, the surfaces brief, CONTEXT.md, and the instructions JSON.

# Product

<!-- impeccable:product-schema 1 -->

## Scope

App-wide only. Every statement in this file must be true of all of LSAT U.

A rule that binds one surface does not belong here. It goes in that surface's brief under `.impeccable/surfaces/`. This split is deliberate and is the correction to a real failure: the previous PRODUCT.md was written on 2026-05-02 for the Intro to LR bootcamp alone, then widened app-wide on 2026-05-08 in a commit whose stated purpose was adding messenger principles. Bootcamp anti-references silently became binding on the drills, and stayed binding for four months. A one-surface rule written into an app-wide file has no boundary that can hold it. Keeping the file app-wide only removes the mechanism.

The repo already carries eight bounded contexts with their own glossaries (`CONTEXT-MAP.md`). Surface-specific product truth follows those boundaries, not this file.

## Platform

web

## Users

**Primary: Aspiring Attorneys clients.** Students who are paying Joshua for one-to-one LSAT coaching. They have consultations, scheduled sessions, transcripts, assigned work, and a real back-and-forth with him. The platform is where their coaching lives between sessions. They arrive already knowing who their coach is, and design decisions are made for them first.

**Secondary: independent self-study learners.** Students using the platform without an active coaching relationship. They are served by the same surfaces but are not the audience a tradeoff is resolved in favour of.

**Operator: Joshua.** The sole instructor and the product, voice, and content authority. He assigns work, publishes material, reviews student activity, and answers messages. He works from any device at any hour, so operator surfaces are as responsive as student surfaces.

13	## Platform
14	
15	web
16	
17	## Users
18	
19	**Primary: Aspiring Attorneys clients.** Students who are paying Joshua for one-to-one LSAT coaching. They have consultations, scheduled sessions, transcripts, assigned work, and a real back-and-forth with him. The platform is where their coaching lives between sessions. They arrive already knowing who their coach is, and design decisions are made for them first.
20	
21	**Secondary: independent self-study learners.** Students using the platform without an active coaching relationship. They are served by the same surfaces but are not the audience a tradeoff is resolved in favour of.
22	
23	**Operator: Joshua.** The sole instructor and the product, voice, and content authority. He assigns work, publishes material, reviews student activity, and answers messages. He works from any device at any hour, so operator surfaces are as responsive as student surfaces.
24	
25	## Product Purpose
26	
27	LSAT U is the operational platform for Joshua's coaching practice. It delivers the method, holds the student's work, and keeps the coaching relationship live between sessions.
28	
29	For a student: one place that knows what they have done, what they got wrong, and what to do next.
30	
31	For Joshua: one workspace to assign, publish, review, and reply without losing the one-to-one feel that his practice is sold on.
32	
33	Success is a student who logs in without being chased, knows the next action without asking, and can point to what improved.
34	
35	## Positioning
36	
37	**A real coach is in it.**
38	
39	The claim a competing product cannot truthfully copy is that a named human being is on the other side of the platform, has met this student, and has looked at this student's work. Assignments come from him. Messages are answered by him. The material was authored by him. Nothing in the product should be built in a way that would still be true if the coach were removed, and nothing should imply a coach is present where one is not.
40	
41	## Operating Context
42	
43	The platform sits inside a coaching practice that runs partly outside it. Future work must not assume the app is the whole system.
44	
45	- Coaching sessions happen on video and are transcribed. Session records, notes, and recaps originate outside the app.
46	- Booking and payment run through their own flow (packages, booking passes, a public booking microsite, single sessions).
47	- Students also practice on third-party platforms. Work done elsewhere is real work, and the app is not the only record of a student's effort.
48	- Joshua authors material ahead of time and assigns it, rather than the system generating it on demand.
49	- Students use both desktop and mobile, in focused study blocks and in short check-ins.
50	
51	## Capabilities and Constraints
52	
53	Confirmed surfaces: authentication and onboarding, the foyer, the classroom, guided drills and the question bank, full-passage Reading Comprehension, the Activity Centre, the Testing Centre and practice test runs, the wrong answer journal, saved sets, analytics and history, scheduling and booking, the inbox, the bootcamps, and an admin and teacher workspace.
54	
55	Technical constraints:
56	
57	- React, TypeScript, Vite, Tailwind, shadcn/ui and Radix primitives, React Router, TanStack Query, Supabase.
58	- One motion library (`framer-motion`). One primary icon library (`lucide-react`). New work composes existing primitives before introducing a new dependency.
59	- Deployment is not a side effect of pushing to `main`. Production at lsatprep.study requires an explicit publish step.
60	- Database migrations in `supabase/migrations/` are an archive, not an applied ledger. Production schema changes go through their own path, and UI must not assume a table exists because a migration file does.
61	
62	Terminology is not defined here. It is defined per bounded context and mapped by `CONTEXT-MAP.md`. A word can carry different weight in different contexts, and collapsing them has already caused real bugs (a **flag** is not a **wrong answer**; **Set** and **Position** were separated for this reason).
63	
64	## Brand Commitments
65	
66	- **Name:** LSAT U, operating under Aspiring Attorneys. Production domain lsatprep.study.
67	- **No em-dashes in anything a student or prospect reads.** Joshua does not type them. A stray em-dash reads as machine-written and costs trust. This binds computed copy, utility copy, and authored copy alike.
68	- **Voice: undecided, deliberately.** The previous record carried a "coach voice, not industry voice" principle and two named voice registers (R1 procedural, R2 metaphor-led). Both were removed on 2026-09-05 by Joshua's decision, on the grounds that they were not stated clearly enough to be usable. They are being redone. Until a replacement is written, no work should cite the old voice rules, and no work should invent a voice rule to fill the gap. Route student-facing copy to Joshua.
69	
70	## Evidence on Hand
71	
72	Real:
73	
74	- A live production deployment at lsatprep.study with real enrolled students.
75	- Authored LSAT curriculum: bootcamp modules, guided drill banks, RC passages with per-question explanations, and practice test content.
76	- Real student work in the database: attempts, sessions, flags, wrong answers, assignments.
77	- A real named instructor with a real coaching practice behind the product.
78	
79	Absent, and never to be fabricated:
80	
81	- No testimonials, case studies, press coverage, score-improvement statistics, or named customer references exist in this repo. Do not write them into any surface.
82	- No pricing, licensing, or availability claims are established here.
83	- No third-party or copyright framing anywhere in student-facing copy.
84	
85	## Product Principles
86	
87	1. **Truthfulness over performance.** Every number, badge, streak, status, and progress claim traces to real stored data. An honest empty state beats invented content. If nothing has been assigned, the product says so and stops. This is the one principle carried forward unchanged from the previous record, and it is the hardest of the five.
88	
89	2. **Compute the next step; do not present a grid.** A student arriving at a surface should be told what to do, not shown a menu and left to choose. A card grid is what a product offers when it does not know the student.
90	
91	3. **Attempt before explanation.** The student commits to an answer before the product explains anything. Passive reading does not build the skill. Reveal copy addresses what the student actually chose, not a generic key.
92	
93	4. **A measurement is never a gate.** Drills and checks measure after teaching. A miss is framed as the next thing to work on, not a grade or a failure. Nothing in the product rewards attendance rather than work.
94	
95	5. **The coach is not simulated.** Where the product speaks for Joshua, it is because Joshua said it. Where he has not, the product does not fill the silence with something that sounds like him. This is the operational form of the positioning claim.
96	
97	## Accessibility & Inclusion
98	
99	**WCAG 2.1 AA is a binding floor across every surface.** It is a defect gate, not an aspiration. A finding below the AA bar is a bug and is fixed, not argued.
100	
101	- Text contrast is at least 4.5:1 against its actual rendered background, and at least 3:1 for large text and for the visual boundaries of interactive controls. Students read dense argument text on this platform for hours at a time.
102	- Every interactive element has a visible focus indicator.
103	- `prefers-reduced-motion` is respected. Motion is never load-bearing for understanding or for completing a task.
104	- Interactive targets meet the 44x44px minimum, and meet it by being that size rather than by a side effect such as wrapped text.
105	- Screen readers are informed of state that changes without a page load: unread counts, send confirmations, and live updates.
106	- Contrast is verified against what actually renders in the browser, not against the token values a theme is assumed to apply. The theme layer has shipped a light card on a near-black page while the token file read as correct.

241	
242	# Design System: LSAT Academy
243	
244	## Overview
245	
246	**Creative North Star: "LSAT Academy"**
247	
248	One institution with two registers, and the seam between them is deliberate.
249	
250	**The Hall** is the door. Unauthenticated, dark, and material: near-black ground, warm marble text, a bronze that carries the light, and a serif that behaves like carved lettering. Its own source calls it a museum nocturne, carved marble filmed in darkness, read by modern instruments. Its job is to make a prospective student feel they are entering somewhere, not signing up for something. It is the only place in the product where atmosphere outranks throughput.
251	
252	**The Workroom** is everything past sign-in. Light, neutral, and quiet, because a student sits inside it for an hour reading dense argument text and the interface must not compete with the passage. There is no warmth here and that is the decision, not an oversight: warm accents need a dark ground to read, and on the Workroom's white they collapse. The Hall's bronze measures 8.58:1 on its own night and 2.30:1 on the Workroom's page. The two registers do not share a palette because they cannot.
253	
254	What they do share is restraint about meaning. In both, colour is a signal. Green, red and amber say something happened. Nothing is coloured to be warm, to look premium, or to fill a space. Depth comes from hairlines and background tone rather than shadow, and hierarchy comes from size, weight and position rather than hue.
255	
256	Inside the Workroom, individual rooms may commit fully to their own world. The eight Activity Centre worlds, the scoped bootcamps and the inbox each carry a painted identity behind a wrapper class. That is sanctioned and it is what an academy is: neutral corridors, characterful rooms. The corridor never takes on a room's paint.
257	
258	**Key Characteristics:**
259	- Two registers, one institution: a dark marble Hall at the door, a light neutral Workroom inside.
260	- Colour means something or it is absent.
261	- Flat surfaces, hairline separation, no drop shadows.
262	- Precise and quiet controls that stay out of the reader's way.
263	- Rooms may be worlds; corridors never are.
264	
265	## Colors
266	
267	Two grounds, one shared vocabulary of meaning.
268	
269	### Primary
270	
271	- **Workroom Ink** (`#121212`): the Workroom's near-black. It is the text colour, the fill of every primary button, and the colour of the focus ring. The Workroom has no accent, so ink carries emphasis by itself.
272	- **Hall Bronze** (`#c6a467`): the Hall's warm light. Hairline borders on the call to action, a sheen across it on hover, and the selection highlight. It exists only on the Hall's night ground and never crosses into the Workroom.
273	
274	### Secondary
275	
276	- **Hall Cyan** (`#8ad8e2`): the Hall's cool instrument note against the bronze, and the colour of its focus outlines. Rare by design; it marks precision where bronze marks warmth.
277	- **Teacher Amber** (`#c7841f`): the single accent of the teacher workspace, with a hue-locked ramp for hover (`#af7318`), text on tinted fills (`#895910`) and a light step (`#e0a648`). Deliberately theme-independent: the workspace is always the light format. It is the one accent in the product, and it belongs to the operator, not the student.
278	
279	### Tertiary
280	
281	Individual rooms carry their own palettes behind a wrapper class and are documented in their own surface briefs, not here. The wrappers in use are `.classroom-root`, `.mc-bootcamp`, `.ff-bootcamp`, `.rc-bootcamp`, `.topic-abstract`, `.cs-punk`, and the eight Activity Centre worlds.
282	
283	`.classroom-root` was added to this list on 2026-09-08, after an audit found the classroom running on Outfit and a warm palette while this record still described it as neutral Workroom chrome. It is a sanctioned world, not drift: the warmth and the typeface are deliberate and they are what makes the classroom feel like a study rather than a dashboard. The record was wrong, not the code. Its brief is `.impeccable/surfaces/classroom.md`. Note that this is the one sanctioned world that is also a corridor, so the rule below about corridors never being worlds now has exactly one documented exception.
284	
285	### Neutral
286	
287	- **Workroom Page** (`#fcfcfc`) and **Card** (`#ffffff`): the page ground and the surface that sits on it. One step of separation, no more.
288	- **Workroom Raised** (`#f7f7f7`) and **Fill** (`#f2f2f2`): the two tonal steps available for layering and for resting fills.
289	- **Workroom Ink Muted** (`#737373`): secondary text. Measures 4.62:1 on the page, which clears the floor with almost nothing to spare. Do not lighten it.
290	- **Workroom Hairline** (`#e0e0e0`) and **Field** (`#e6e6e6`): the borders that do the work shadows used to.
291	- **Hall Marble** (`#eae5da`), with secondary (`#a6a093`) and muted (`#6e695e`) steps: warm off-white text on the night ground.
292	
293	### State (semantic only, never decorative)
294	
295	- **Correct** (`#10b77f`) and **Incorrect** (`#f43e5c`): a student's attempt was right or wrong. Held under their own names, separate from system status, so a future shift to one cannot silently reframe the other.
296	- **Warn** (`#f59f0a`): a bonus, a stretch, a non-blocking notice.
297	- **Success** (`#21c45d`), **Warning** (`#f59f0a`), **Destructive** (`#dc2828`): system status, not student performance.
298	
299	### Named Rules
300	
301	**The Ground Decides Rule.** A warm colour is legible on the Hall's night and illegible on the Workroom's page. Never carry a colour across the seam on the strength of liking it; measure it against the ground it will land on first.
302	
303	**The Meaning-Only Rule.** A colour appears in the Workroom because something is right, wrong, cautionary, or dangerous. There is no fifth reason. If a surface feels flat and the impulse is to add a colour, the answer is type and space.
304	
305	**The Named Ground Rule.** No colour is written as a literal hex in a component. It resolves from a token, and a token that resolves to nothing is a defect, not a subtle effect. Three colour names in this codebase pointed at variables that were never defined, and the product silently lost its focus ring for five months.
306	
307	## Typography
308	
309	**Workroom Body:** Lexend (with Outfit, then the system stack)
310	**Hall Display:** Fraunces (with Iowan Old Style, Times New Roman, serif)
311	**Hall Inscription:** Cinzel (with Trajan Pro, serif)
312	**Mono:** IBM Plex Mono
313	**Question Lock:** Outfit, held deliberately for LSAT question surfaces
314	
315	**Character:** The Workroom is one humanist sans doing everything, sized and weighted rather than swapped. The Hall is a serif institution: an optical serif for its headings and an inscriptional face for its few carved lines. The split is the whole typographic idea. Inside, type gets out of the way; at the door, type is the architecture.
316	
317	### Hierarchy
318	
319	- **Title** (600, 24px, line-height 1, tracking -0.025em): card and section headings in the Workroom.
320	- **Body** (400, 14px rising to 16px at 640px and above, line-height 1.6): everything a student reads in the Workroom chrome.
321	- **Question** (400, 16px, line-height 1.6, Outfit): LSAT stimulus and answer choices. Font-locked to Outfit even though the rest of the app moved to Lexend, so question text does not shift when the shell's face changes.
322	- **Label** (500, 14px): button and control labels.
323	- **Hall Display** (380, fluid, tracking -0.015em, Fraunces): Hall headings. The unusually light 380 weight is the point; it reads as carved rather than printed.
324	- **Hall Inscription** (500, tracking 0.05em, Cinzel): the Hall's few short carved lines. Never body copy.
325	- **Hall Mono** (400, 13px, tracking 0.04em, IBM Plex Mono): the Hall's instrument labels.
326	
327	### Named Rules
328	
329	**The One Face Rule.** The Workroom uses a single family. Hierarchy is built from size, weight and space. A second family inside the Workroom is a defect unless it is the Outfit question lock.
330	
331	**The Question Never Moves Rule.** LSAT question text is font-locked. A change to the shell's typeface must not reflow a passage a student is mid-way through reading.
332	
333	**The Serif Stays At The Door Rule.** Fraunces and Cinzel belong to the Hall. A serif appearing in the Workroom chrome means the seam has leaked.
334	
335	## Layout
336	
337	The Workroom is a centred single column with generous side gutters. The container centres by default, carries 32px of horizontal padding, and stops widening at 1400px.
338	
339	Breakpoints are the framework defaults up to 1280px, with the largest step pulled in to 1400px. Body type steps from 14px to 16px at 640px, and form controls are forced to 16px below 768px so that iOS does not zoom the page when a field takes focus. Named breakpoints `phone` (up to 600px), `tablet` (601 to 900px) and `desktop` (901px and up) exist for bootcamp surfaces.
340	
341	Spacing runs on a 4px base. The steps in real use are 4, 8, 16, 24 and 32. Card padding is 24px on all sides; button padding is 8px vertical and 16px horizontal.
342	
343	Reading measure matters more here than in most products. Passage and stimulus text is held to a comfortable line length rather than allowed to run the full container width.
344	
345	### Named Rules
346	
347	**The Reading Measure Rule.** Any surface carrying an LSAT passage, stimulus, or explanation constrains its own line length. The container's maximum is a ceiling for layout, never a licence for prose.
348	
349	## Elevation & Depth
350	
351	**Flat, with hairline separation.** Surfaces sit on the page rather than above it. A card is distinguished from its ground by a one-pixel border and, where more is needed, by one step of background tone. There are no drop shadows in the Workroom.
352	
353	This is a decision, not a description. The codebase currently ships five shadow tokens tuned for a dark ground, from `0 1px 3px rgba(0,0,0,0.4)` up to `0 8px 32px rgba(0,0,0,0.7)`, and applies the smallest of them to every card. Because the app ships light by default, those shadows land at 40 percent black under a white card. The light theme never redefined them, and the one glow token is white, so it is invisible on white. The tokens are retired by this record and the code has not caught up yet.
354	
355	The Hall is the exception and keeps its own depth: fixed ambience gradients, a grain overlay, and an inset highlight on its call to action. That depth is atmospheric rather than structural, and it does not transfer.
356	
357	### Named Rules
358	
359	**The Flat Rule.** Depth in the Workroom comes from a hairline or a tonal step. A `box-shadow` on a Workroom surface is a defect.
360	
361	**The Two-Step Rule.** Page, then card, then at most one raised step. A surface that needs a fourth level of layering is a surface that needs restructuring.
362	
363	## Shapes
364	
365	Corners are gently rounded and consistent: 8px for cards and containers, 6px for buttons and inputs, 4px for the smallest chips and insets, and a full pill only for genuine badges. The scale derives from one 0.5rem radius token, so a change to the base moves the whole system together.
366	
367	The Hall uses a near-square 2px radius instead. Its edges are cut, not softened, which is what separates a carved surface from a card.
368	
369	Borders are one pixel. There is no second border weight in the Workroom.
370	
371	### Named Rules
372	
373	**The Hairline Rule.** One pixel, always. A thicker border used as a coloured accent stripe on the leading edge of a surface is banned, including when faked with an inset shadow. Active state is carried by background tone.
374	
375	## Components
376	
377	### Buttons
378	
379	- **Character:** precise and quiet. A button is a plain rectangle with a label. It darkens slightly on hover and does nothing else. It does not lift, does not cast a shadow, and does not scale on press.
380	- **Shape:** gently rounded (6px).
381	- **Primary:** ink fill (`#121212`), page-coloured label, 8px by 16px padding.
382	- **Outline:** page background, one-pixel field border, ink label.
383	- **Ghost:** no fill at rest, a tonal fill on hover.
384	- **Sizes:** default 44px tall, small 40px, large 48px, icon 44 by 44. Every size clears the 44px touch minimum by being that height, never by relying on wrapped text or padding accidents.
385	- **Focus:** a solid two-pixel ink outline, offset two pixels from the edge. This is the only focus treatment in the Workroom.
386	- **Hall CTA:** 60px tall, near-square corners, transparent fill over a bronze hairline, with a bronze sheen that sweeps across on hover and a cyan outline on focus.
387	
388	### Cards / Containers
389	
390	- **Corner:** 8px.
391	- **Background:** card white on the page ground.
392	- **Border:** one-pixel hairline. This is what separates the card, not a shadow.
393	- **Shadow:** none.
394	- **Padding:** 24px, with header, content and footer sharing that inset.
395	
396	### Inputs / Fields
397	
398	- **Style:** page background, one-pixel field border, 6px corners, 44px minimum height, 8px by 12px padding.
399	- **Focus:** the same two-pixel ink outline as buttons, offset two pixels. Do not express focus as a border colour change alone; a border shift is too quiet to serve as the only indicator.
400	- **Mobile:** 16px text below 768px, so focusing a field never zooms the viewport.
401	
402	### Navigation
403	
404	Neutral chrome throughout. The sidebar runs one tonal step off the page ground with a hairline edge, and the active item is marked by background tone rather than by a coloured rule on its leading edge.
405	
406	### Signature Component: the scoped world
407	
408	The product's distinctive pattern is not a control, it is a container. A room commits fully to its own palette, typefaces and atmosphere behind a single wrapper class, and nothing inside it leaks out. Scene layers are `aria-hidden` furniture painted beneath the content, so the accessible tree is identical whether the room is painted or bare. A new world is added by writing a new wrapper and its prefixed tokens, never by adding hex to a shared component.
409	
410	## Do's and Don'ts
411	
412	### Do:
413	
414	- **Do** resolve every colour from a token, and treat a token that resolves to nothing as a defect. Verify a new token renders before documenting it.
415	- **Do** give every interactive element a visible focus indicator: a solid two-pixel ink outline offset two pixels in the Workroom, cyan in the Hall.
416	- **Do** check contrast against the ground the colour will actually land on, in the theme that actually ships, in a browser. The theme file has read as correct while the page rendered a light card on a near-black ground.
417	- **Do** separate surfaces with a one-pixel hairline and one tonal step.
418	- **Do** keep colour semantic: correct, incorrect, warning, danger. Nothing else earns it.
419	- **Do** scope a new room's identity behind its own wrapper class with prefixed tokens, the way the bootcamps and the eight Activity worlds already do.
420	- **Do** hold LSAT passage and stimulus text to a readable line length, and leave it on the Outfit question lock.
421	- **Do** end every world's stylesheet with a `prefers-reduced-motion` block that stops continuous motion rather than speeding it up.
422	
423	### Don't:
424	
425	- **Don't** add a `box-shadow` to a Workroom surface. Depth is hairline and tone.
426	- **Don't** carry a Hall colour into the Workroom. Bronze reads 8.58:1 on the Hall's night and 2.30:1 on the Workroom's page.
427	- **Don't** introduce a warm or metallic accent into the Workroom to make it feel premium. Premium here is depth, hierarchy and type.
428	- **Don't** remove the browser's focus outline without shipping a replacement in the same rule. `outline-none` with no working ring is worse than leaving the default alone.
429	- **Don't** animate the focus indicator. A `transition-all` on a control means the ring fades in, which delays the one signal a keyboard user depends on.
430	- **Don't** use a border thicker than one pixel as a coloured accent stripe, including when faked with an inset shadow.
431	- **Don't** put a second typeface into the Workroom chrome, and don't let a serif cross in from the Hall.
432	- **Don't** animate layout properties. Animate `opacity`, `transform` and `color`. No bounce, no elastic, no lift on hover.
433	- **Don't** write a hex value into a component. If a room needs its own colour, promote it to named tokens behind that room's wrapper.
434

=====
## Read, Recall, Colour door (locked 2026-09-05)

The two RRC doors (`SectionLibraryLaunch`, `SectionLaunch`, both drawn by `SectionLaunchFrame`) are a committed world of their own, mode **Persuade** at the top and **Operate** in the slab. Joshua's brief: a layered, high-end landing page in the spirit of heylemon.ai (cream ground, painted panels, serif display with italic emphasis, mono micro-labels, a dictation demo) with fora.so's depth-plane hero, and bold step 1 to 4 instructions.

- **World:** one dusk. The runner's indigo (`#1a1635`) lifted into a sky over a painted meadow, cream paper (`#f6f3ea`) beneath. Display is Fraunces at opsz 144; body Inter; labels IBM Plex Mono. Marigold marks the Colour step, emerald marks a carried detail, nothing else is coloured.
- **Hero:** `MeadowScene` paints five depth planes generatively in SVG (seeded ridges, seeded grass blades, feTurbulence grain) so no raster ships; each plane rides `useScroll` at its own rate and the foreground grass crosses in front of the demo card. The headline blurs and recedes on the same scroll value.
- **Signature moment:** `RecallDemo`, a looping performance of the method on a synthetic stimulus, labelled as such, whose text literally dissolves at the Recall step.
- **Steps:** four cards, each with a small drawn scene, doubling as the ARIA tablist the tests already contract (`sl-beat`, `sl-beat-tab-N`, `sl-beat-panel`). The full briefing is the second tab of the drawer.
- **Tokens:** scoped to `.sl` as `--rd-*`. The design detector reports them as outside DESIGN.md; that is the sanctioned-world exception above, not drift.

====REPORT
## The Report Card (locked 2026-09-08)

Its own world, scoped to `.rc` in `report-card.css`. Joshua's brief: highly
organised, easy to run through, high-end, with her recording and the standard's
side by side and the transcript following the sound. Explicitly **not cream, not
emerald, and not grey** ("the cream paper colour is overdone and too AI
looking", "I don't want emerald", "it should be much more colorful"). The second
sanctioned departure from the wing's surface after the RRC door.

- **Two grounds.** A deep plum console (`oklch(0.245 0.058 312)`) holds the
  verdict, the transport and the ledger; a violet-cast white page
  (`oklch(0.979 0.008 312)`) holds everything she reads at length.
- **Colour means a KIND of feedback, never a severity.** The mechanic is
  Grammarly's, which colours by category and reserves red for outright errors.
  Four fixed tones: **Detail** plum-rose, **Read** teal, **Colour** gold,
  **Method** indigo. A miss is the colour of the thing it is a miss of, which is
  what stops twenty-two stimuli of corrections reading as a heat map of failure.
- **Signal magenta is sound, not a category.** It marks the line currently
  playing and nothing else, held at hue 328 so it separates from the
  plum-rose dropped chips it shares a line with.
- **Hues chosen from research, not taste** (Firecrawl, 2026-09-08). Saturated
  right now: acid yellow, electric cobalt, orange and coral, warm sand and
  terracotta, black plus one neon accent. Under-used: violet and plum, soft
  cyan, dusty rose as a field, complex cool neutrals. Green and cream are the
  owner's veto; orange-red was this file's first answer and is on the over-used
  list. The neutrals are tinted toward violet the way Material 3's baseline is
  (`#fffbfe` paper, `#7d5260` plum tertiary), a system built to hold AA.
- **Band is fill, not hue.** The ledger's marks differ by how full they are, in
  gold; a recital is dashed and a lost one is a hollow rose ring. It survives
  grayscale, which is what lets category own the colour.
- **One stimulus at a time.** Twenty-two accordion rows became a ledger of
  twenty-two marks plus one open stimulus in a fixed order: the passage, what
  you held, what slipped, your Read, line by line, what you said, next time.
- **No Detail arithmetic on screen.** A Detail is our grading unit and she has
  never seen one enumerated, so "20 of 21 details" is gone: `howMuch()` says
  "Almost all of it came back" instead (Joshua, 2026-09-08).
- **Contrast is measured, not assumed.** `scripts/_rc_contrast.mjs` converts
  computed colours through a canvas (Chrome serialises them as `oklch()`) and
  every text node clears 4.5:1 on its own ground, in the default, playing and
  clean states.
- **Type ramp:** Fraunces display, Lexend body, IBM Plex Mono labels. The design
  detector reports the sizes as outside DESIGN.md; that is this sanctioned world,
  not drift, the same exception the RRC door's `--rd-*` tokens carry.
- **Harness:** `/src/dev/report-card-preview.html` renders it at twenty-two
  stimuli without `App.tsx`. `?state=clean|faults|partial`, `?playing=N` to
  freeze the playhead on a line. `scripts/_rc_shots.mjs` captures the matrix.

# Activity Centre — surface brief

## Scope and mode

The Activity Centre wing: the home list (`/activities`), each Activity's intro, and the eight worlds that fill the runner's left column. Mode: **Experience** for the worlds (the student is inside the room the skill lives in), **Operate** for the answer loop on the right, which stays on the module's cream surface untouched.

## Direction (locked 2026-08-21)

**Eight worlds, eight universes.** Every Activity opens into a fully committed physical scene, saturated and material, scoped behind its wrapper class so nothing leaks into the shell. This is a deliberate, owner-approved departure from the app-wide monochrome-editorial restraint: the Centre is the one wing where the room IS the teaching instrument. The Coach and answer loop remain the constant; the room changes.

| World | Activity | Scene | Display face | Key hues |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Polarity Chamber | Negation Station | obsidian void split by an aurora; survey-grid floor; prismatic glass plate | Italiana | ultraviolet 275°, polar cyan 190° |
| Specimen Lab | Vocab Rehab | Victorian naturalist's study; green wall, parchment bench, bell jar on brass plinth | EB Garamond | botanical green 162°, brass 40°, madder 8° |
| Signal Observatory | Spot the Relationship | celestial cartographer's dome; nebulae, twinkling stars, gilt ribs | Marcellus | indigo 233°, gold 45°, comet cyan 187°, rose 330° |
| Switchyard | Conditional Rephrase | midnight rail yard; sodium floods, fog, converging track fan, solari board | Barlow Condensed | steel-night 213°, sodium 36°, signal emerald 158° |
| Argument Atelier | Find the Conclusion | stained-glass workshop; lamp cone, dust, tinted panes in lead cames | Young Serif | umber 24°, lamp gold 40°, glass tints rotating |
| Debate Chamber | Rebuttal Rephrase | torchlit porphyry rotunda; bronze protractor inlaid in the floor | Cinzel | porphyry 356°, torchlight 36°, bronze 30° |
| The Press | Dumb it Down | hot-metal print shop; ink rollers, iron frame, vermilion proof red | Bevan | iron 20°, paper cream 42°, vermilion 14° |
| The Loom | Spot the Callback | indigo dye-house; warp threads, linen sheets with selvage, saffron thread | Alegreya | indigo 236°, linen 43°, saffron 42° |

Statement/body text across dark worlds runs Spectral 300; micro-labels stay on the shared `--ac-mono`.

## Standing rules

- **Scene layers are furniture, not content.** Every world's atmosphere lives in one `aria-hidden` layer stack (`.po-room`, `.sl-room`, …) painted below `z-index: 2` content; tested DOM never gains or loses nodes for scenery.
- **Meaning colors survive grayscale.** Hue is atmosphere on top of geometry/weight/position, never the only carrier of a verdict. Emerald still means "earned/true" wherever it appears.
- **One orchestrated entrance per world**, durations pinned by tests (1800–2500ms), always skippable, `useReducedMotion` short-circuits to `onDone()`.
- **Reduced motion**: every world CSS ends with a `prefers-reduced-motion` block; continuous loops stop rather than speed up.
- **The home is a hall of doorways.** Each `ActivityCard` carries a pure-CSS diorama of its world (`activity-scenes.css`, keyed by slug); the card's facts stay on the module paper below the scene. The intro borrows `--act-accent` from its world via the registry.
- **Registry accents** (`worlds/registry.tsx`) are each world's signature hue and feed the card eyebrow + intro accent.

## Unresolved

- Negation Station is not in `SEED_ACTIVITIES`, so its diorama only shows once assigned.
- Progress ornaments (chips, plaques, mileposts, seals…) remain unreachable from the runner; feeding them real verdicts is its own piece of work.

=====CONTEXT
### Read, Recall, Colour

The four-step vocabulary of the RRC runner, plus the language its grading uses.
The source method's own words for these steps are listed under `_Avoid_` and are
never used in the product.

**Chunk**:
An authored cut of a stimulus, roughly a sentence, carrying the Details that fall inside it. A guide for her eye and the unit content is written in, never a boundary she is held to: she reads, recalls and colours in whatever units she likes.
_Avoid_: Line, segment, piece, part

**Read** (step 1):
The silent reading of a chunk. Nothing is spoken.
_Avoid_: Skim, study

**Retelling** (step 2):
Saying a chunk back out loud in her own words. **Retelling** is the word we use to each other and in code; the label a student reads is always **Tell it back**.
_Avoid_: Reading Recall (its former name, which put "Recall" on two different steps), Say it back (the old student label), translate, translation, paraphrase, summary, repeat-back

**Colouring In** (step 3):
Her spoken reaction to the chunk she just recalled — a thought, doubt, example, or joke that fits that chunk and no other.
_Avoid_: Quip, comment, annotation, reaction

**The Recall** (step 4):
The end of a stimulus, worked with the text gone: she says the whole argument back from memory.
_Avoid_: The Read (its former name, which collided with step 1), Retelling (that is step 2), CLIR, recap, rebound, takeaway

**Detail**:
One of the small authored facts a stimulus contains. PT102.S3.Q1 holds 21.
_Avoid_: Point, fact, bullet, item (reserved for an Activity's prompt)

**Coverage**:
How many of a stimulus's Details she carried, counted across everything she said about it rather than against any one Chunk.
_Avoid_: Accuracy, completeness, recall rate

**Reciting**:
Saying a stimulus back close to word-for-word. A failure of the Retelling, not a step of its own: Coverage looks perfect and nothing was understood.
_Avoid_: Parroting, copying, verbatim recall, memorising

**Listing**:
Delivering a stimulus as a flat run of facts rather than a story in her own words. The opposite failure to Reciting: Coverage looks perfect and the argument was never held together.
_Avoid_: Shopping list, itemising, enumerating

**Report Card**:
What a finished section produces: every stimulus graded on its own merits, showing what she missed and what she got perfect, with her own recording playable beside it. One player per stimulus, never per question, because two questions can share a stimulus and she recorded it once.
_Avoid_: Scorecard (informally taken by the MCQ Drill results screen), Verdict (that is the coach judging live, which this surface never does), results, grade, review

**Clip**:
One stimulus's worth of her recording, playable on the Report Card. A Clip is cut out of the single unbroken section recording after she finishes; it is never recorded as its own file, because the drill forbids stopping the microphone mid-section.
_Avoid_: Take, snippet, segment (that is the timing record the Clip is cut from), track

**Attempt**:
One complete run of one section by one student. A section may be attempted any number of times, and every Attempt keeps its own date, its own recording and its own Report Card. Nothing is overwritten and nothing is hidden once done.
_Avoid_: Try, retry, session (reserved for a tutoring session), run

**Ungraded**:
A stimulus the judge never answered for, because the call to it failed. Distinct from a stimulus she said nothing about, which is a real zero: this one has no measurement at all. It is excluded from Coverage and from every count of her habits, because reporting our outage as her behaviour is worse than reporting nothing.
_Avoid_: Failed, missing, unscored, zero, error

**Sweep**:
The scheduled re-drive of a finished section that never got a grade, or got a partial one. The client asks for grading the moment she stops and does not wait on the answer; the Sweep is what makes that ask safe to lose. A closed tab never costs a Report Card.
_Avoid_: Retry, cron, job, backfill, reconcile

### The section launch surface

The page a student meets before a section run, and the parts of it that carry
names. Nothing here is a phase of the runner; these are things drawn on one
page.

**Section Launch**:
The page a student lands on before running an RRC section: the method as four Beats, her last few Attempts, and the one control that starts the run. `SectionLaunch` in code, `.sl-` in CSS. It replaces the editorial intro for section mode only; the eight typed Activities keep theirs.
_Avoid_: The Chamber (the approved mockup's name for it, and already a world under `worlds/chamber/`), Homepage (that is an Activity's own intro page), intro, splash, lobby, dashboard

**Beat**:
Internal code term for one of the four method steps shown on Section Launch. It may remain in implementation names, but it is never student-facing. Students see **step**: Read it, Tell it back, Colour it, Recall it.
_Avoid in student-facing copy_: Beat, beats, phase, tab, card

**Tick**:
One mark in the row that draws a past Attempt: one per stimulus slot, placed at its authored `item_order` rather than its array position. Four states, because three would lie — `recorded`, `notJudged` (the judge never answered, so the mark is hollow), `silent` (she spoke nothing into that stimulus), `missing`.
_Avoid_: Dot, pip, square, cell, bar

**Target**:
The clock the section was meant to fit, taken from the assignment's time goal at her resolved accommodation. It measures elapsed time and nothing else, and a run is only ever compared against a Target equal to its own.
_Avoid_: Goal (`within_goal` in the database also carries method conditions, which Target deliberately excludes), limit, par, deadline, pace

          "Do not give back the gist, or the summary, or the important parts. You cannot know which parts were important until you have read the whole thing, so filtering on the way through mostly means quietly throwing out the piece you were going to need.",
          "Fewer words is fine when the words cover the same ideas. Fewer ideas is not. The names, the numbers, and the small words that carry the load (all, most, only, probably) either come back or they do not.",
          "**Efficiency is never a licence to leave information out.**"
        ],
        "heading": "Simpler is not the same as shorter."
      },
      {
        "body": [
          "You will open your mouth to colour something and find nothing there. That lasts about a week.",
          "It is not that you have no thoughts. It is that nobody has ever asked you for one in the middle of reading before, and the habit takes a few sessions to wake up. Say something obvious on purpose if you have to. \"Okay, sure.\" \"That seems like a stretch.\" \"Who measured that?\" All of those count.",
          "The reaction is what makes the sentence yours instead of the author's. Skip it and you are back to repeating words you do not own."
        ],
        "heading": "You will think you have nothing to say. Everybody does."
      },
      {
        "body": [
          "While you are working the stimulus, nothing on the screen hides. The whole thing stays readable, which means the only thing stopping you rereading a line while you tell it back is you, looking away from it.",
          "That is deliberate. Reading while you recall is a one way street with cars coming both ways. It jams. You stop using your memory at all and slide straight back into the author's exact words instead of finding your own.",
          "It is also the version that still works on paper, and on test day. A screen that hides the text for you is a crutch that cannot come with you."
        ],
        "heading": "Look away. That part is on you."
      },
      {
        "body": [
          "The goal is twenty-five minutes for a whole section, and it only counts if every stimulus got recalled, coloured, and given The Recall. Finishing early with four of them skipped is not finishing early. If you have extra time on test day, your goal scales with it.",
          "The clock in the corner shows how long you have been going. It is there so you can pace a section. It never counts down, because a number falling away in the corner of your eye produces exactly the skating read this drill exists to fix.",
          "It is not a race. It is the pace at which this stops being a study exercise and starts being how you read under a clock. Go slower while it is new. The speed shows up on its own once the method does."
        ],
        "heading": "Twenty-five minutes, and what that number means."
      }
    ]
  }
}
